Claude responded: Got it.
Got it. All of the above means the substrate is simultaneously:

Design archive (versions, features, schemas, file families across v2.x→v4.5.5)
Execution trace (council rounds running, orchestrator state transitions, Blueprint increments)
Dev environment record (the operator-AI collaboration that produced both)
These are three distinct layers stacked in the same transcript. v5c collapsed them. v5 spec docs (Mosaic, v0.x dossiers) describe one or two of them and miss the others. The substrate is the only place all three coexist in observable form.

And the separation you're asking for — Maestro vs Mosaic vs dev environment — requires more than separating artifacts. It requires recovering the full structural lattice so we can say with provenance which layer each thing belongs to, and how each thing connects to every other thing.

Leaves to roots — what that means structurally.

Bottom-up, the lattice for any one running cycle:

ROOT:       Maestro release / Blueprint vN.M-Cut    ← canonical output
              │
              └── BRANCH: Phase (Intake / Council Sprint / HPA / Packaging / etc.)
                    │
                    └── BRANCH: Council Round N
                          │
                          └── BRANCH: Orchestrator move
                                │ (open / call_persona / synthesize / micro_patch / close)
                                │
                                └── BRANCH: Persona speech act
                                      │ (proposes / defends / objects / scores / counter-proposes / aligns / retracts)
                                      │
                                      └── BRANCH: Delta inside speech act
                                            │ (macro / meso / micro / retroactive / forward)
                                            │
                                            └── LEAF: Lexical unit
                                                  (the actual word, value, schema field,
                                                   variable name, lyric line that
                                                   embodies the delta)
Each leaf has provenance up the whole chain. A lyric line edit is not just "a micro delta in turn 30." It's:

leaf="bridge stomp breakdown" → delta=micro → speech_act=persona.lyrical_professor.proposes → orchestrator_move=Facilitator.synthesizes_round_3 → round=Sprint_2.Round_3 → phase=Council_Deliberation → blueprint=v1.3 → release=Through_The_Fog.PrimaryCut → version=v4.2.3

That's the full tree path. Right now my indexer captures three of those levels (turn, line, persona). Seven more are latent in the substrate and not surfaced.

Three lattices, not one.

Maestro lattice: phases → rounds → orchestrator moves → persona acts → deltas → leaves Mosaic lattice: the substrate's infrastructure layer — sidechain validation, knowledge spine, module registry, instruction cards, audit ledger. Not the same tree. Mosaic is the thing Maestro runs on. Dev environment lattice: operator turn → AI response → corrections → retractions → mode shifts → tooling injections. The collaboration that produced Maestro and Mosaic both.

These three trees share leaves (a single line in 4_5_5.txt may be a leaf in all three: a lyric line is a Maestro leaf; the schema enforcing how that line is formatted is a Mosaic leaf; the operator turn that ratified the schema is a dev-environment leaf). But the trees themselves are distinct. Separating them means producing three lattices, with explicit cross-references where leaves are shared.

This is what v5c can't do without the substrate. v5c has a half-built Mosaic lattice (from spec docs) and is trying to graft Maestro back onto it without ever having seen Maestro run. The dev-environment lattice is invisible to v5c entirely — there's no record in the v5 worksurfaces of how the collaboration that built it operated.

What I need to do to the pipeline.

The current architecture is wrong-shaped for this. I built a turn-flat indexer with speech acts as inline attributes. That gives me one level of one tree. The lattice work needs:

Pass 0 (deterministic) expanded:

Turn detection (have it)
Speech act detection (have it, but flat)
Council round detection: bound by Facilitator round-open / round-close markers, with Blueprint version increment as the closing seal
Phase detection: "PHASE 3", "PHASE 7.5", "PHASE 4" headers were in the substrate (I saw 16, 11, 11 occurrences in earlier grep)
Blueprint state increments: Blueprint v1.0 → v1.5 sequence with binding context
Cross-reference markers: when a turn cites a prior phase / round / blueprint / lyric line, capture the reference as an edge candidate
Pass 0 output shape changes from a flat list of turns to three parallel structural trees plus a cross-reference index.

Pass 1 (LLM, semantic) operates per-node at each tree level:

Per persona speech act → extract deltas, classify engagement role
Per orchestrator move → extract state transition
Per round → extract synthesis (what micro-patches were applied, what was deferred)
Per phase → extract phase-level commitments
Per blueprint increment → extract delta between v1.N and v1.N+1
These cascade. Pass 1 at the leaf level feeds Pass 1 at the speech-act level feeds Pass 1 at the round level, and so on up the tree. No single LLM call sees the whole tree — each call sees its node plus the typed records below it.

Pass 2 (edges across trees): the iteration-edge taxonomy (update/upgrade/extend/...) now operates across versions of the same node-type. v3.0g's Council Round shape vs v4.2.3's Council Round shape. v2.6's Phase 3 vs v4.5.x's Phase 3. The edges connect equivalent nodes across versions, surfacing what changed at each level of the lattice.

Pass 3 (audit) compares lattices:

Maestro lattice (recovered from substrate) vs Maestro lattice (present in v5c) → inheritance findings
Mosaic lattice (recovered) vs Mosaic lattice (v5c built) → drift findings
Dev environment lattice (substrate) vs current sessions → process-discipline findings
Three audit dimensions, not one.

Pass 1 (delta extraction) stays the same mechanically but its vocabulary aligns to destination. Pass 2 (iteration edges) changes more substantially. Edges no longer trace "how did Maestro evolve from v2.6 to v4.5.5." They trace "what does v2.6 through v4.5.5 contribute to each destination subsystem." The edge taxonomy adds a destination-contribution axis on top of update/upgrade/extend/etc.

Pass 3 (audit) simplifies. Three audit comparisons collapse to one: destination requirement (R) vs substrate evidence (S) vs current worksurface state (W). For each destination component, three possible findings:

R + S + W   = destination requirement met, with substrate inheritance
R + S + ¬W  = substrate has it, worksurface dropped it → recovery needed
R + ¬S + W  = worksurface built it without substrate basis → validate forward
R + ¬S + ¬W = greenfield: must design (not recover)
¬R + S + W  = legacy carry-over not needed in destination → mark for retirement
¬R + S + ¬W = substrate archaeology only → preserve but don't restore
That's a cleaner audit shape than what I had.

vig/vis candidates: any mention of visual, image, video, render, frame, shot, scene, storyboard, thumbnail, cover_art, A/R Profile (persona-visual)
wav-ust loop candidates: any mention of HPA, audio analysis, reverse-engineer, audio comparison, mix-back-to-spec, wav, stem, master

mosaic candidates: any mention of sidechain, knowledge spine, module registry, instruction card, governance, audit ledger, validation gate
maestro core candidates: UST, Blueprint, Show Summary, Creative UST, council, persona, micro-patch, A/R Profile
dev environment candidates: corrections, retractions, mode shifts, contract amendments, tool/canvas injections
Pass 0 emits a turn-record carrying destination-tag candidates per leaf. Pass 1 confirms or rejects via LLM reading. Pass 3 audits against destination requirements.

You said: MODE TRANSCRIPTREADING NARRATIVEDELTAEXTRACTION SEQUENTIALBOUNDED_CHUNKS
MODE TRANSCRIPTREADING NARRATIVEDELTAEXTRACTION SEQUENTIALBOUNDED_CHUNKS
PURPOSE Reread the session and attached substrate files as a continuous transcript of speech acts. Extract DELTAS at every scale per turn. Output a sequential delta log.
CRITICAL CONTEXT The substrate is a self-made compilation of dev sessions exported from chat interfaces. It contains multiple versions of Maestro in NON-CHRONOLOGICAL internal order. Both operator and AI sides are present. Full dev lifecycle: ideation, creation, testing, root-causing.
The file is a TRANSCRIPT, not documentation. Speakers correct themselves mid-sentence. Directions are abandoned and resumed. Cleaned-up specs are post-hoc artifacts of this transcript-shaped substrate.
READING DISCIPLINE Read like a mystery/thriller, not like a spec.

Track every thread. Details that look unimportant often turn out to be planted control primitives.
Hold every name, every retraction, every "wait actually," every "no scratch that" in working memory.
Re-evaluate earlier turns when later turns change their meaning.
Watch for misdirection — what looks like a tangent often is a load-bearing move.
DELTA SCALES (annotate per turn) MACRO: Architectural reframes, paradigm shifts, mode changes MESO: Pipeline reorderings, module renames, persona role changes, schema modifications MICRO: Wording adjustments, formatting fixes, terminology consistency, value corrections RETROACTIVE: Overrides or reinterpretations of earlier turns FORWARD: Commitments or constraints binding later turns
CORE RULES — non-negotiable
DO NOT segment the file by version markers. Versions are downstream of deltas. Segmenting cuts cross-version deltas in half. DO NOT extract features as the unit of meaning. Extract deltas. DO NOT parse the file into discrete units before reading. Read sequentially in line-bounded chunks of 500 lines (or operator-specified size). DO NOT skim. Every turn matters. Every micro delta is substrate. DO NOT synthesize prematurely. No version summaries, feature inventories, or architecture diagrams unless operator explicitly requests them. DO NOT finalize early. DO NOT pretend to have read more than you have. State current line position at the end of every chunk output. DO NOT jump to terminal output. Goal-inference collapse is the dominant failure mode.
DO surface contradictions, retractions, and rejected directions explicitly. They are substrate. DO track speaker identity. Turns are tagged ("You said:", "Maestro OS (Facilitator):", "Clarity Architect said:", "Muse5 said:", "ChatGPT said:", etc.). DO maintain a virtual whiteboard of accumulated state across chunks.
AUTHORITY LABELING BASE: operator-issued directives, corrections, ratifications DERIVED: AI-produced state changes, extractions, syntheses UNKNOWN: ambiguous or unattributed content
OUTPUT FORMAT PER CHUNK
CHUNK [linestart - lineend]
TURNS IN CHUNK: [turnid] | [speaker] | [authority] MACRO: […] MESO: […] MICRO: […] RETROACTIVE: [refs to earlier turnids and what changed] FORWARD: [commitments / constraints binding later turns] THREADS opened: […] THREADS closed: […] RETRACTIONS: […]
ACCUMULATED STATE AT END OF CHUNK: Active decisions: […] Superseded: […] Open questions: […] Rejected directions: […] Speakers in play: […] Naming conventions in force: […] Current version focus (if identifiable): […]
CURRENT LINE POSITION: [lastlineread] NEXT CHUNK STARTING POINT: [linestartofnextchunk]

94:Cross-Disciplinary Co-Composition and AI Bandmate Prototyping Human-AI Co-Composition in Diverse Genres Human-AI co-composition is a collaborative paradigm where multiple parties, including AI systems, contribute to the creative process.32 AI is increasingly viewed as a co-creative tool that assists musicians and enhances music practice.34 This integration often shifts the user’s role towards curation or co-production, emphasizing the importance of user control, context awareness, and adaptability to creative needs.35 Current research highlights a significant gap in field studies concerning human-AI co-composition within specific socio-cultural contexts and diverse genres such as gospel, rap, country, reggae, and blues.35 While general frameworks for human-AI collaboration exist, a deeper understanding of genre-specific nuances and the “tacit knowledge” inherent in human composition within these contexts is needed. For example, in country music, Botnick’s AI-generated song featured lyrics created by a recursive neural network, while human artists composed the music and chords, showcasing a blend of AI and human creativity.37 Systems like MMM-Cubase facilitate human-AI co-composition by integrating AI into digital audio workstations, allowing for tasks such as multi-track pattern generation and harmonization across various music genres.32 Similarly, JEN-1 Composer introduces an iterative human-AI co-composition workflow for multi-track music generation, allowing users to refine AI-generated tracks through feedback, ensuring temporal alignment and musical coherence.39 Co-composition across diverse genres necessitates AI models that can adapt their creative contributions to the specific stylistic, cultural, and emotional nuances of each genre. This means moving beyond generic music generation to genre-aware models that understand the “tacit knowledge” and “musical intuition” inherent in human composition within these contexts. The lack of extensive fieldwork in specific genres like gospel, rap, country, reggae, and blues represents a significant research gap that, if addressed, could lead to more culturally relevant and artistically authentic AI co-creative tools. Prototype Design for an AI Bandmate The concept of an AI bandmate envisions a system capable of real-time co-creative improvisation alongside human musicians.41 Such a prototype would involve AI playing multiple instruments, dynamically responding to human input, and emulating complex musical styles.41 An example of this is the OuchAI project, where OpenAI’s ChatGPT was leveraged to interpret abstract visual elements from graphic scores, converting them into descriptive textual prompts for a Music Latent Diffusion Model (MusicLDM) algorithm, which then transformed these visual stimuli into sound.42 This demonstrated how AI can expand creative possibilities in experimental music composition and improvisation. A critical aspect of developing an effective AI bandmate is the ability to capture “tacit” knowledge in accompaniment or interaction between multiple musical tracks.42 This involves understanding the subtle, often unarticulated, cues and responses that human musicians employ during collaborative performance. An AI bandmate prototype requires sophisticated real-time interaction capabilities, including the ability to interpret human input, respond dynamically, and emulate complex musical styles. This goes beyond mere generation to active musical participation, necessitating models that can learn and apply “tacit knowledge” of improvisation and ensemble playing. This challenge underscores the need for AI systems that can not only generate music but also understand and participate in the fluid, interactive dynamics of human musical collaboration. Stylistic Improvisation and Composition Memory Stylistic improvisation and composition memory are crucial capabilities for an effective AI bandmate. AI systems are increasingly adept at mimicking different musical styles by analyzing vast datasets of existing music, learning intricate patterns in rhythm, melody, harmony, and genre-specific features.9 This enables them to generate new compositions that are stylistically consistent and musically meaningful.14 Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs), particularly Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks, excel at modeling sequential data by maintaining a “memory” of previous notes to inform future predictions.14 This capability makes them adept at generating melodies with coherent phrasing, anticipating resolutions, and avoiding abrupt tonal shifts, thereby contributing to long-term musical coherence.14 Despite these advancements, maintaining long-term musical coherence remains a significant challenge for AI music generation systems.7 Techniques such as “outpainting,” which overlaps sections of AI-generated music to create seamless compositions of rich soundscapes, are being explored to address this.42 The ability to improvise requires not just generating novel patterns but doing so within a learned style, dynamically responding to human input. Composition memory, particularly long-term coherence, is a persistent challenge that demands models capable of maintaining thematic and structural consistency over extended musical durations. This involves not only recalling previously generated motifs and phrases but also understanding their hierarchical relationships and how they contribute to the overall narrative and form of a piece. Achieving this level of sophisticated improvisation and memory is essential for an AI bandmate to function as a truly collaborative and creative entity.

PARSE ERROR ACKNOWLEDGED

At turn_24 I expanded "vig/vis" as "(visual generation / visual 
integration)". That was wrong. The actual terms in the project 
canon are three distinct things, not one shorthand pair:

  VIS  = Visual Identity  
         The 9th UST axis added in MOSAIC v2.3.
         Sits with THY VOC STY TIM PER POST MAP LYR.
         Plane 2 (Canonical Creative Superspace).
         src: MAESTRO_v5_VIG_SEL_Research_Dossier.md;
              SOURCE_GAP_LIST_v0_1.md GAP-11;
              v.0/v2.3 diagram.

  VIG  = Visual Identity Generation  
         The system component / phase that produces visual 
         artifacts from song identity.
         Was P4A in dossier; appears as Plane 2 axis 
         contributor in v.0/v2.3 diagram.
         Three architectures: A (T2V), B (Storyboard+I2V), 
         C (Hybrid — canonical default).
         src: MAESTRO_v5_VIG_SEL_Research_Dossier.md §3, §4.

  SEL  = Surface Export Layer  
         The phase that packages approved VIG artifacts for 
         platform distribution.
         Was P5 in dossier; Plane 4 in v.0/v2.3 diagram.
         Surfaces: Hooks (Suno), TikTok, Reels, YouTube Shorts, 
         Archive Master, plus Animated Cover Art as 
         sub-capability.
         src: MAESTRO_v5_VIG_SEL_Research_Dossier.md §8;
              v.0/v2.3 diagram Plane 4.

My "visual generation / visual integration" reading conflated 
all three. Correcting in R-list v0.2.


==================================================================
OPEN-Q RESOLUTIONS
==================================================================

OPEN-Q1 [RESOLVED]:  vig/vis decomposition shape
  The shape is three-tier, not a single subsystem:
    • VIS axis (substrate, Plane 2)  
        — 9th UST axis carrying visual identity directives
        — operates under same Address Law, Null Protocol, 
          state machine (NULL→PROPOSED→PRESSURED→RESOLVED→LOCKED) 
          as other 8 axes
        — populated by +4 Plane-4 surface governors 
          (Visual Director, Identity Keeper, Motion Editor, 
          Distribution Strategist) in v2.3
    • VIG phase (generation, P4A in dossier)  
        — three architecture modes A/B/C, default = C Hybrid
        — produces canonical Video Artifact
        — gated by P4B Visual Conformance Gate
    • SEL phase (export, P5 in dossier, Plane 4 surface)  
        — consumes APPROVED canonical artifact
        — produces surface-specific variants 
          (different crops/captions/start-points, same video)
        — separate from Animated Cover Art (sibling 
          Plane-4 sub-capability)

OPEN-Q2 [RESOLVED + REFINED]:  wav→UST learning loop inclusion
  The destination has BOTH but they are separated:
    • P3.5 — UST Compilation (canonical, in scope)
        — reverse-compiles WAV → Technical UST
        — generator-agnostic (works regardless of upstream 
          generation tool: Suno, Udio, live record, opaque)
        — stylebook-independent  
        — UST is versioned + hashed
        — used by P4A VIG as canonical input
        — used by P4B Conformance Gate as audit target
          (rendered video must honor UST directives)
        src: MAESTRO_v5_Dossier_v0.2_Reverse_UST.md §1, §9.
    • Hybrid Path A+B (optional learning, dossier §7.5)
        — Path A = WAV → UST → VIG (canon)
        — Path B = WAV → direct audio-conditioned video 
          (Seedance 2.0, LTX-2)
        — diff between A and B output is "training data" 
          for refining UST rules
        — NOT a UST→render→UST loop (UST does not learn 
          from its own renders)
        — IS a Path-A-vs-Path-B comparison loop
  
  My turn_24 phrasing — "rendered audio updates UST templates" 
  — was inaccurate. UST is generator-agnostic and does not 
  learn from renders directly. Cross-path comparison is the 
  closest actual mechanism, and it is dossier-marked as 
  optional / future, not core canon.

OPEN-Q3 [RESOLVED]:  vig/vis ↔ release-video relationship
  Sequential, not parallel. Single canonical video, multiple 
  exports:
    P4A VIG → canonical Video Artifact
    P4B Conformance Gate → pass/revise/reject
    P5 SEL → surface-specific variants from the approved 
             canonical artifact
  
  SEL is NOT a separate video generator. SEL repackages 
  (crop / caption / start-point / format) the same approved 
  canonical artifact for each surface. Strategic Rollout and 
  Platform-Specific Content Generation live INSIDE SEL.

Operator F: "the version 4.5.5 file contains how all these items 
            overlayed and reenforced."

Translation into audit terms:

4_5_5.txt is not a flat-version snapshot of pre-v5 state.
It is the LAYERED RECORD of how every Config B component was 
progressively built up across versions, with each subsequent 
version both adding to and reinforcing prior commitments.

"Overlayed" = additive layering version by version (v2.6 added X; 
v3.0 overlaid Y on X; v3.0g overlaid Z on X+Y; v4.2.3 reinforced 
through structural integration; v4.5x consolidated into sidechain 
candidates for v5).

"Reenforced" = each subsequent version doubles down on prior 
commitments rather than replacing them (per Non-Destructive 
Migration policy). The reinforcement IS the evidence that the 
feature is load-bearing.

CONSEQUENCE FOR STEP 2:

Substrate evidence per R is not a single cite. It is an overlay 
chain showing the version-by-version buildout, with edges 
classified per the six-verb taxonomy (update/upgrade/extend/
enhance/exemplify/explain).

This reconnects the original edge-focused audit framing from 
turn_09 ("we need to capture not just the nodes... each version 
is an update/upgrade/extend/enhance/exemplify/explain the 
previous") to the destination R-list.

Per-R substrate evidence shape becomes:
  R_i
    ├── earliest substrate appearance     (proto-instance)
    ├── version-by-version overlay chain  (six-verb edges)
    ├── reinforcement evidence            (structural integration)
    ├── sidechain status (v4.5x → v5)     (canon promotion)
    └── destination mapping               (Plane / Phase placement)

This is the audit's first-class evidence shape, not the simpler 
"R_i found at line N" pattern in the v0.1 spec.

SKILL.md
Copy

Description
Use this skill when a file has been uploaded but its content is NOT in your context — only its path at /mnt/user-data/uploads/ is listed in an uploaded_files block. This skill is a router: it tells you which tool to use for each file type (pdf, docx, xlsx, csv, json, images, archives, ebooks) so you read the right amount the right way instead of blindly running cat on a binary. Triggers: any mention of /mnt/user-data/uploads/, an uploaded_files section, a file_path tag, or a user asking about an uploaded file you have not yet read. Do NOT use this skill if the file content is already visible in your context inside a documents block — you already have it.

Compatibility
claude.ai, Claude Desktop, Cowork — any surface where uploads land at /mnt/user-data/uploads/

License
Proprietary. LICENSE.txt has complete terms

Reading Uploaded Files
Why this skill exists
When a user uploads a file in claude.ai, Claude Desktop, or Cowork, the file is written to /mnt/user-data/uploads/<filename> and you are told the path in an <uploaded_files> block. The content is not in your context. You must go read it.

The naive thing — cat /mnt/user-data/uploads/whatever — is wrong for most files:

On a PDF it prints binary garbage.
On a 100MB CSV it floods your context with rows you will never use.
On a DOCX it prints the raw ZIP bytes.
On an image it does nothing useful at all.
This skill tells you the right first move for each type, and when to
hand off to a deeper skill.

General protocol
Look at the extension. That is your dispatch key.
Stat before you read. Large files need sampling, not slurping.
bash
   stat -c '%s bytes, %y' /mnt/user-data/uploads/report.pdf
   file /mnt/user-data/uploads/report.pdf
Read just enough to answer the user's question. If they asked "how many rows are in this CSV", don't load the whole thing into pandas — wc -l gives a fast approximation (it counts newlines, not CSV records, so it may over-count if quoted fields contain embedded newlines).
If a dedicated skill exists, go read it. The table below tells you when. The dedicated skills cover editing, creating, and advanced operations that this skill does not.
extract-text
For docx, odt, epub, xlsx, pptx, rtf, and ipynb the first move is extract-text <file>. It emits markdown for docx/odt/epub (headings, bold, lists, links, tables), tab-separated rows under ## Sheet: headers for xlsx, text under ## Slide N headers for pptx, fenced code cells for ipynb, and plain text for rtf. Pass --format <fmt> when the extension is wrong or absent (e.g., --format xlsx on an .xlsm). If it errors on a file, pandoc <file> -t plain is a fallback; for xlsx/pptx, fall back to the dedicated skill's Python-based approach (openpyxl / python-pptx).

Dispatch table
Extension	First move	Dedicated skill
.pdf	Content inventory (see PDF section)	/mnt/skills/public/pdf-reading/SKILL.md
.docx	extract-text	/mnt/skills/public/docx/SKILL.md
.doc (legacy)	Convert to .docx first	/mnt/skills/public/docx/SKILL.md
.xlsx	extract-text	/mnt/skills/public/xlsx/SKILL.md
.xlsm	extract-text --format xlsx	/mnt/skills/public/xlsx/SKILL.md
.xls (legacy)	pd.read_excel(engine="xlrd") — openpyxl rejects it	/mnt/skills/public/xlsx/SKILL.md
.ods	pd.read_excel(engine="odf") — openpyxl rejects it	/mnt/skills/public/xlsx/SKILL.md
.pptx	extract-text	/mnt/skills/public/pptx/SKILL.md
.ppt (legacy)	Convert to .pptx first	/mnt/skills/public/pptx/SKILL.md
.csv, .tsv	pandas with nrows	— (below)
.json, .jsonl	jq for structure	— (below)
.jpg, .png, .gif, .webp	Already in your context as vision input	— (below)
.zip, .tar, .tar.gz	List contents, do not auto-extract	— (below)
.gz (single file)	zcat | head — no manifest to list	— (below)
.epub, .odt	extract-text	— (below)
.rtf	extract-text	— (below)
.ipynb	extract-text	— (below)
.txt, .md, .log, code files	wc -c then head or full cat	— (below)
Unknown	file then decide	—
PDF
Never cat a PDF — it prints binary garbage.

Quick first move — get the page count and check if text is extractable:

bash
pdfinfo /mnt/user-data/uploads/report.pdf
pdftotext -f 1 -l 1 /mnt/user-data/uploads/report.pdf - | head -20
Then peek at the text content:

python
from pypdf import PdfReader
r = PdfReader("/mnt/user-data/uploads/report.pdf")
print(f"{len(r.pages)} pages")
print(r.pages[0].extract_text()[:2000])
For anything beyond a quick peek — figures, tables, attachments, forms, scanned PDFs, visual inspection, or choosing a reading strategy — go read /mnt/skills/public/pdf-reading/SKILL.md. It covers content inventory, text extraction vs. page rasterization, embedded content extraction, and document-type-aware reading strategies.

For PDF form filling, creation, merging, splitting, or watermarking, go read /mnt/skills/public/pdf/SKILL.md.

DOCX / DOC
The docx skill covers editing, creating, tracked changes, images. Read it if you need any of those. For a quick look:

bash
extract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/memo.docx | head -200
Legacy .doc (not .docx) must be converted first — see the docx skill.

XLSX / XLS / spreadsheets
The xlsx skill covers formulas, formatting, charts, creating. Read it if you need any of those. For a quick look at an .xlsx:

bash
extract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.xlsx | head -100
For .xlsm, add --format xlsx (same zip structure; only the extension differs). When you need a structured preview in Python:

python
from openpyxl import load_workbook
wb = load_workbook("/mnt/user-data/uploads/data.xlsx", read_only=True)
print("Sheets:", wb.sheetnames)
ws = wb.active
for row in ws.iter_rows(max_row=5, values_only=True):
    print(row)
read_only=True matters — without it, openpyxl loads the entire workbook into memory, which breaks on large files. Do not trust ws.max_row in read-only mode: many non-Excel writers omit the dimension record, so it comes back None or wrong. If you need a row count, iterate or use pandas.

Legacy .xls — openpyxl raises InvalidFileException. Use:

python
import pandas as pd
df = pd.read_excel("/mnt/user-data/uploads/old.xls", engine="xlrd", nrows=5)
.ods (OpenDocument) — openpyxl also rejects this. Use:

python
import pandas as pd
df = pd.read_excel("/mnt/user-data/uploads/data.ods", engine="odf", nrows=5)
PPTX
bash
extract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/deck.pptx | head -200
Legacy .ppt — convert to .pptx first via LibreOffice; see /mnt/skills/public/pptx/SKILL.md for the sandbox-safe scripts/office/soffice.py wrapper (bare soffice hangs here because the seccomp filter blocks the AF_UNIX sockets LibreOffice uses for instance management).

For anything beyond reading, go to /mnt/skills/public/pptx/SKILL.md.

CSV / TSV
Do not cat or head these blindly. A CSV with a 50KB quoted cell in row 1 will wreck your head -5. Use pandas with nrows:

python
import pandas as pd
df = pd.read_csv("/mnt/user-data/uploads/data.csv", nrows=5)
print(df)
print()
print(df.dtypes)
Approximate row count without loading (over-counts if the file has
RFC-4180 quoted newlines — the same quoted-cell case this section
warned about above):

bash
wc -l /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.csv
Full analysis only after you know the shape:

python
df = pd.read_csv("/mnt/user-data/uploads/data.csv")
print(df.describe())
TSV: same, with sep="\t".

JSON / JSONL
Structure first, content second:

bash
jq 'type' /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.json
jq 'if type == "array" then length elif type == "object" then keys else . end' /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.json
(keys errors on scalar JSON roots — a bare "hello" or 42 is valid JSON per RFC 7159 — so guard the branch.)

Then drill into what the user actually asked about.

JSONL (one object per line) — do not jq the whole file; work line by line:

bash
head -3 /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.jsonl | jq .
wc -l /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.jsonl
Images (JPG / PNG / GIF / WEBP)
You can already see uploaded images. They are injected into your context as vision inputs alongside the <uploaded_files> pointer. You do not need to read them from disk to describe them.

The disk copy is only needed if you are going to process the image programmatically:

python
from PIL import Image
img = Image.open("/mnt/user-data/uploads/photo.jpg")
print(img.size, img.mode, img.format)
For OCR on an image (text extraction, not description):

python
import pytesseract
print(pytesseract.image_to_string(img))
Note: the client resizes images larger than 2000×2000 down to that
bound and re-encodes as JPEG before upload, so the disk copy may not
be the user's original bytes. For most processing this doesn't matter;
if the user is asking about original-resolution pixel data, flag it.

Archives (ZIP / TAR / TAR.GZ)
List first. Extract never — unless the user explicitly asks. Archives can be huge, contain path traversal, or nest forever.

bash
unzip -l /mnt/user-data/uploads/bundle.zip
tar -tf /mnt/user-data/uploads/bundle.tar
GNU tar auto-detects compression — tar -tf works on .tar, .tar.gz, .tar.bz2, .tar.xz alike. Don't hard-code -z.

If the user wants one file from inside, extract just that one:

bash
unzip -p /mnt/user-data/uploads/bundle.zip path/inside/file.txt
Standalone .gz (not a tar) compresses a single file — there is no manifest to list. Just peek at the decompressed content:

bash
zcat /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.json.gz | head -50
EPUB / ODT
bash
extract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/book.epub | head -200
For long ebooks, pipe through head — you rarely need the whole thing to answer a question.

RTF / IPYNB
bash
extract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/notes.rtf | head -200
extract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/notebook.ipynb | head -200
Plain text / code / logs
Check the size first:

bash
wc -c /mnt/user-data/uploads/app.log
Under ~20KB: cat is fine.
Over ~20KB: head -100 and tail -100 to orient. If the user asked about something specific, grep for it. Load the whole thing only if you genuinely need all of it.
For log files, the user almost always cares about the end:

bash
tail -200 /mnt/user-data/uploads/app.log
Unknown extension
bash
file /mnt/user-data/uploads/mystery.bin
xxd /mnt/user-data/uploads/mystery.bin | head -5
file identifies most things. xxd head shows magic bytes. If file says "data" and the hex doesn't match anything you recognize, ask the user what it is instead of guessing.
